My mind keeps going from “Will I ever get hired again?” to “Have I ruined my reputation with parents?” and then “Am I falling behind on retirement?” Even little things, like if I made a mistake during story time, I start wondering if that means I shouldn’t be trusted with children at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless or on edge much of the time?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t really paid attention to whether I’m on edge in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control the worrying once it starts?\n\nPatient: Yes, very hard. I’ll try to redirect myself—make a list, pray, go weed the garden, focus on the next practical step—but it doesn’t really stop. One worry just hands off to another, and then I realize I’ve lost half the afternoon to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your worrying is out of proportion to the actual problems you’re facing?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is out of proportion. It’s hard to shut it off.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling more easily fatigued than usual?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 53 / preschool_or_kindergarten_teacher / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Honestly, what finally made me come in is how tense my body is all the time. My shoulders and neck stay clenched all day, like I’m bracing for a classroom observation that never ends. By the evening my jaw aches so much I can barely enjoy playing guitar.\n\nDoctor: Did this constant muscle tension start after a particular stressful event?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it started after I got fired from the preschool. It’s been going on for about a year and two months now.\n\nDoctor: Does the tension get noticeably worse when something reminds you of being fired from the preschool?\n\nPatient: It’s pretty much there all the time, honestly. My shoulders and neck stay clenched through the day, like I’m bracing for something, and by evening my jaw aches so much I can hardly enjoy playing guitar.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: Yes. It jumps from one thing to another. I worry about whether I’ll ever get hired again, whether my reputation with parents is ruined, whether I’m falling behind on retirement… and then even small things, like if I made a mistake during a story-time activity, I start thinking maybe I shouldn’t be trusted with children at all. It just keeps going.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, is it hard for you to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Yes. I try to stop it—I’ll tell myself to make a list, or pray, or go weed the garden, or just focus on the next practical thing. But it doesn’t really shut off. One worry just turns into the next, and before I know it I’ve lost half the afternoon.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries feel mainly tied to losing your preschool job?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes, it starts there a lot. But it doesn’t stay just on losing the job. My mind keeps going from “Will I ever get hired again?” to “Have I ruined my reputation with parents?” and then “Am I falling behind on retirement?” Even little things, like if I made a mistake during story time, I start wondering if that means I shouldn’t be trusted with children at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless or on edge much of the time?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I feel tense a lot, but I don’t know if I’d call it restless. I haven’t really paid attention to whether I’m on edge in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control the worrying once it starts?\n\nPatient: Yes, very hard. I’ll try to redirect myself—make a list, pray, go weed the garden, focus on the next practical step—but it doesn’t really stop. One worry just hands off to another, and then I realize I’ve lost half the afternoon to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your worrying is out of proportion to the actual problems you’re facing?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is out of proportion. And then it can go to something small, like a mistake during story-time, and I start wondering if that means I shouldn’t be trusted with children. It just keeps moving from one thing to the next.\n\nDoctor: If the preschool firing had never happened, do you think you would still be worrying this much?\n\nPatient: I think the firing really is when it started. My mind just keeps circling—whether I’ll ever get hired again, whether I’ve ruined my reputation with parents, whether retirement is slipping away. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. It’s been going on for about a year and two months now.\n\nDoctor: Does the tension get noticeably worse when something reminds you of being fired from the preschool?\n\nPatient: It’s pretty much there all the time, honestly. My shoulders and neck stay clenched through the day, like I’m bracing for something, and by evening my jaw aches so much I can hardly enjoy playing guitar.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: Yes. It jumps from one thing to another. I worry about whether I’ll ever get hired again, whether my reputation with parents is ruined, whether I’m falling behind on retirement… and then even small things, like if I made a mistake during a story-time activity, I start thinking maybe I shouldn’t be trusted with children at all. It just keeps going.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, is it hard for you to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Yes. I try to stop it—I’ll tell myself to make a list, or pray, or go weed the garden, or just focus on the next practical thing. But it doesn’t really shut off. One worry just turns into the next, and before I know it I’ve lost half the afternoon.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries feel mainly tied to losing your preschool job?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes, it starts there a lot. But it doesn’t stay just on losing the job. My mind keeps going from “Will I ever get hired again?” to “Have I ruined my reputation with parents?” and then “Am I falling behind on retirement?” Even little things, like if I made a mistake during story time, I start wondering if that means I shouldn’t be trusted with children at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless or on edge much of the time?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I feel tense a lot, but I don’t know if I’d call it restless. I haven’t really paid attention to whether I’m on edge in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control the worrying once it starts?\n\nPatient: Yes, very hard. I’ll try to redirect myself—make a list, pray, go weed the garden, focus on the next practical step—but it doesn’t really stop. One worry just hands off to another, and then I realize I’ve lost half the afternoon to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your worrying is out of proportion to the actual problems you’re facing?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is out of proportion. I mean, losing the job is a real problem, but my mind takes it much further. I’ll start with whether I’ll ever get hired again, then I’m worrying that I’ve ruined my reputation with parents, or that I’m falling behind on retirement. And then it can get as extreme as thinking one small mistake during a story-time activity means maybe I shouldn’t be trusted with children anymore. So yes, it feels bigger than the actual problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying on most days about things besides the job loss?\n\nPatient: Yes, most days it goes beyond just the job loss. My mind keeps going from “Will I ever get hired again?” to “Have I ruined my reputation with parents?” and then “Am I falling behind on retirement?” Even little things, like if I made a mistake during story time, I start wondering if that means I shouldn’t be trusted with children at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless or on edge much of the time?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I feel tense a lot, but I don’t know if I’d call it restless. I haven’t really paid attention to whether I’m on edge in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control the worrying once it starts?\n\nPatient: Yes, very hard. I’ll try to redirect myself—make a list, pray, go weed the garden, focus on the next practical step—but it doesn’t really stop. One worry just hands off to another, and then I realize I’ve lost half the afternoon to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your worrying is out of proportion to the actual problems you’re facing?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is out of proportion. I mean, losing the job is a real problem, but my mind takes it much further. I’ll start with whether I’ll ever get hired again, then I’m worrying that I’ve ruined my reputation with parents, or that I’m falling behind on retirement. And then it can get as extreme as thinking one small mistake during a story-time activity means maybe I shouldn’t be trusted with children anymore. So yes, it feels bigger than the actual problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying on most days about things besides the job loss?\n\nPatient: Yes, most days it goes beyond just the job loss. My mind keeps circling—will I ever get hired again, have I ruined how parents see me, am I falling behind for retirement. And then it can go to something small, like a mistake during story-time, and I start wondering if that means I shouldn’t be trusted with children. It just keeps moving from one thing to the next.\n\nDoctor: If the preschool firing had never happened, do you think you would still be worrying this much?\n\nPatient: I think the firing really is when it started. This level of worrying has been going on about a year and two months now, since I was let go from the preschool. Before that, I worried some, but not like this.\n\nDoctor: On most days, do you find yourself worrying for much of the day?\n\nPatient: Yes. On most days it takes up a lot of the day. My mind just keeps circling—whether I’ll ever get hired again, whether I’ve ruined my reputation with parents, whether retirement is slipping away. And then it can jump to something small, like a mistake during story-time, and I start thinking maybe I shouldn’t be trusted with children anymore. It’s hard to shut it off.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more easily fatigued than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed feeling more tired than usual in a clear way. I mean, some days I feel worn out, but I can’t say if it’s more than normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to concentrate because of the worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. When I’m filling out applications, or even trying to plan a simple lesson, I’ll read the same paragraph over and over. My attention keeps jumping to what an administrator might think of me, or whether I missed some important detail. It makes it hard to get through even basic things.\n\nDoctor: Have you been more irritable than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed being more irritable than usual. I might get tense or short in moments, but I can’t say it’s a clear change for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes. Most nights I’m lying there for hours, going over things in my head—like how I answered in an interview, bills, licensing forms, that kind of thing. And even when I do fall asleep, I’ll wake up around four in the morning already tense, thinking through what could go wrong that day.\n\nDoctor: Right now, does your worrying feel mostly tied to the preschool firing, or does it also regularly happen about other parts of life that aren’t connected to that event?\n\nPatient: It started with the firing, but it doesn’t stay there. My mind keeps jumping around—from whether I’ll ever get hired again, to whether I’ve ruined my reputation with parents, to whether retirement is slipping away. And then it can get as small as thinking one mistake during a story-time activity means maybe I shouldn’t be trusted with children at all. It just keeps circling.\n\nDoctor: Before you lost the preschool job, were you someone who worried excessively about lots of different parts of life?\n\nPatient: Before the job loss, I don’t think it was like this. The worrying now is much more constant and it jumps around a lot—whether I’ll get hired again, whether parents think badly of me, whether retirement is slipping away. Even small things, like making a mistake during story time, can turn into me thinking maybe I shouldn’t be trusted with children.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. By the evening my jaw aches so much I can barely enjoy playing guitar.\n\nDoctor: Did this constant muscle tension start after a particular stressful event?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it started after I got fired from the preschool. It’s been going on for about a year and two months now.\n\nDoctor: Does the tension get noticeably worse when something reminds you of being fired from the preschool?\n\nPatient: It’s pretty much there all the time, honestly. My shoulders and neck stay clenched through the day, like I’m bracing for something, and by evening my jaw aches so much I can hardly enjoy playing guitar.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: Yes. It jumps from one thing to another. I worry about whether I’ll ever get hired again, whether my reputation with parents is ruined, whether I’m falling behind on retirement… and then even small things, like if I made a mistake during a story-time activity, I start thinking maybe I shouldn’t be trusted with children at all. It just keeps going.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, is it hard for you to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Yes. I try to stop it—I’ll tell myself to make a list, or pray, or go weed the garden, or just focus on the next practical thing. But it doesn’t really shut off. One worry just turns into the next, and before I know it I’ve lost half the afternoon.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries feel mainly tied to losing your preschool job?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes, it starts there a lot. But it doesn’t stay just on losing the job. My mind keeps going from “Will I ever get hired again?” to “Have I ruined my reputation with parents?” and then “Am I falling behind on retirement?” Even little things, like if I made a mistake during story time, I start wondering if that means I shouldn’t be trusted with children at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless or on edge much of the time?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I feel tense a lot, but I don’t know if I’d call it restless. I haven’t really paid attention to whether I’m on edge in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control the worrying once it starts?\n\nPatient: Yes, very hard. I’ll try to redirect myself—make a list, pray, go weed the garden, focus on the next practical step—but it doesn’t really stop. One worry just hands off to another, and then I realize I’ve lost half the afternoon to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your worrying is out of proportion to the actual problems you’re facing?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is out of proportion. I mean, losing the job is a real problem, but my mind takes it much further. I’ll start with whether I’ll ever get hired again, then I’m worrying that I’ve ruined my reputation with parents, or that I’m falling behind on retirement. And then it can get as extreme as thinking one small mistake during a story-time activity means maybe I shouldn’t be trusted with children anymore. So yes, it feels bigger than the actual problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying on most days about things besides the job loss?\n\nPatient: Yes, most days it goes beyond just the job loss. My mind keeps circling—will I ever get hired again, have I ruined how parents see me, am I falling behind for retirement. And then it can go to something small, like a mistake during story-time, and I start wondering if that means I shouldn’t be trusted with children. It just keeps moving from one thing to the next.\n\nDoctor: If the preschool firing had never happened, do you think you would still be worrying this much?\n\nPatient: I think the firing really is when it started. This level of worrying has been going on about a year and two months now, since I was let go from the preschool. Before that, I worried some, but not like this.\n\nDoctor: On most days, do you find yourself worrying for much of the day?\n\nPatient: Yes. On most days it takes up a lot of the day. My mind just keeps circling—whether I’ll ever get hired again, whether I’ve ruined my reputation with parents, whether retirement is slipping away. And then it can jump to something small, like a mistake during story-time, and I start thinking maybe I shouldn’t be trusted with children anymore. It’s hard to shut it off.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more easily fatigued than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed feeling more tired than usual in a clear way. I mean, some days I feel worn out, but I can’t say if it’s more than normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to concentrate because of the worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. When I’m filling out applications, or even trying to plan a simple lesson, I’ll read the same paragraph over and over. My attention keeps jumping to what an administrator might think of me, or whether I missed some important detail. It makes it hard to get through even basic things.\n\nDoctor: Have you been more irritable than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed being more irritable than usual. I might get tense or short in moments, but I can’t say it’s a clear change for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes. Most nights I’m lying there for hours, going over things in my head—like how I answered in an interview, bills, licensing forms, that kind of thing. And even when I do fall asleep, I’ll wake up around four in the morning already tense, thinking through what could go wrong that day.\n\nDoctor: Right now, does your worrying feel mostly tied to the preschool firing, or does it also regularly happen about other parts of life that aren’t connected to that event?\n\nPatient: It started with the firing, but it doesn’t stay there. My mind keeps jumping around—from whether I’ll ever get hired again, to whether I’ve ruined my reputation with parents, to whether retirement is slipping away. And then it can get as small as thinking one mistake during a story-time activity means maybe I shouldn’t be trusted with children at all. It just keeps circling.\n\nDoctor: Before you lost the preschool job, were you someone who worried excessively about lots of different parts of life?\n\nPatient: Before the job loss, I don’t think it was like this. The worrying now is much more constant and it jumps around a lot—whether I’ll get hired again, whether parents think badly of me, whether retirement is slipping away. Even small things, like making a mistake during story time, can turn into me thinking maybe I shouldn’t be trusted with children.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel keyed up or on edge?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I know I feel tense a lot, but “keyed up” is a little hard for me to judge. I wouldn’t know if that’s the right word for it.\n\nDoctor: Do reminders of being fired from the preschool make your anxiety suddenly spike?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed it as a sudden spike from reminders, exactly. I just know I get anxious when I think about the firing, but I don’t know if it comes on all at once like that."
